=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a string s consisting of digits.
Return the number of substrings of s divisible by their non-zero last digit.
Note: A substring may contain leading zeros.
 
Example 1:

Input: s = "12936"
Output: 11
Explanation:
Substrings "29", "129", "293" and "2936" are not divisible by their last digit. There are 15 substrings in total, so the answer is 15 - 4 = 11.

Example 2:

Input: s = "5701283"
Output: 18
Explanation:
Substrings "01", "12", "701", "012", "128", "5701", "7012", "0128", "57012", "70128", "570128", and "701283" are all divisible by their last digit. Additionally, all substrings that are just 1 non-zero digit are divisible by themselves. Since there are 6 such digits, the answer is 12 + 6 = 18.

Example 3:

Input: s = "1010101010"
Output: 25
Explanation:
Only substrings that end with digit '1' are divisible by their last digit. There are 25 such substrings.

 
Constraints:

1 <= s.length <= 10^5
s consists of digits only.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def countSubstrings(self, s: str) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Option 1:
class Solution:
    def countSubstrings(self, s: str) -> int:
        dp = [[0] * d for d in range(10)]
        ans = 0
        trans = [[[(r * 10 + x) % d for r in range(d)] for x in range(10)] for d in range(10)]
        for ch in s:
            x = ord(ch) - 48
            for d in range(1, 10):
                old = dp[d]
                new = [0] * d
                td = trans[d][x]
                for r, cnt in enumerate(old):
                    if cnt:
                        new[td[r]] += cnt
                new[x % d] += 1
                dp[d] = new
            if x:
                ans += dp[x][0]
        return ans

Let's maybe write code with local variables for speed:
class Solution:
    def countSubstrings(self, s: str) -> int:
        dp = [[0] * d for d in range(10)]
        trans = [[[(r * 10 + x) % d for r in range(d)] for x in range(10)] for d in range(10)]
        ans = 0
        for ch in s:
            x = ord(ch) - 48
            for d in range(1, 10):
                old = dp[d]
                new = [0] * d
                td = trans[d][x]
                for r, cnt in enumerate(old):
                    new[td[r]] += cnt
                new[x % d] += 1
                dp[d] = new
            if x:
                ans += dp[x][0]
        return ans

...

This avoids modulo. Fine.

Now, let's consider if we should use `range(1, 10)` for d in trans precompute to avoid trans[0] and maybe dp[0]? Let's write clean code:

class Solution:
    def countSubstrings(self, s: str) -> int:
        dp = [None] + [[0] * d for d in range(1, 10)]
        trans = [None] + [
            [[(r * 10 + x) % d for r in range(d)] for x in range(10)]
            for d in range(1, 10)
        ]
        ans = 0
        for ch in s:
            x = ord(ch) - 48
            for d in range(1, 10):
                old = dp[d]
                new = [0] * d
                td = trans[d][x]
                for r, cnt in enumerate(old):
                    new[td[r]] += cnt
                new[x % d] += 1
                dp[d] = new
            if x:
                ans += dp[x][0]
        return ans

This avoids dp[0] empty. Good.

Could also precompute `add = [x % d for x in range(10)]` for each d? Actually `new[x % d]` inside loop; modulo 9*1e5=900k, trivial. But we can precompute `single = [[x % d for x in range(10)] for d in range(10)]` and use `new[single[d][x]] += 1`. Not necessary.

Before final, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "commit-implement-recheck" instruction maybe they want not just code but reasoning? But final instruction says output only code. We'll follow final.

Let's maybe think about if there is a possible issue with "divisible by their non-zero last digit" and substring "0" if last digit zero, not counted. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "A substring may contain leading zeros" and our DP's singleton addition for x=0: It treats "0" as a substring ending at current position with value0. For future, when appending a non-zero digit, "0" + digit = digit, which is correct for leading zero. For future, when appending another zero, "0"+"0" = "00" value0, correct. Good.

Let's maybe think about if there is a possible issue with "substring may contain leading zeros" and numeric value of "00" is 0, but if later append "1", "001" value1. Our recurrence: "00" value0 -> *10+1 =1.
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
class Solution:
    def countSubstrings(self, s: str) -> int:
        dp = [None] + [[0] * d for d in range(1, 10)]
        trans = [None] + [
            [[(r * 10 + x) % d for r in range(d)] for x in range(10)]
            for d in range(1, 10)
        ]

        ans = 0
        for ch in s:
            x = ord(ch) - 48

            for d in range(1, 10):
                old = dp[d]
                new = [0] * d
                td = trans[d][x]

                for r, cnt in enumerate(old):
                    new[td[r]] += cnt

                new[x % d] += 1
                dp[d] = new

            if x != 0:
                ans += dp[x][0]

        return ans
```
